# Sophicly Table of Techniques — Reference

_Generated from the Table of Techniques prototype (245 techniques). Do not edit by hand — regenerate with `build-techniques-md.js`._

Each entry gives the technique’s definition, worked examples, and effect on the reader (Focus / Feel / Think / Act). Some carry expanded collections (e.g. the Coming-of-Age archetypal journeys, or Want vs Need goals).

## Story Archetypes & Plot

### The Everyman `Ev`

The archetype of ordinariness, and a mask the protagonist wears so the reader sees themselves in him. His common name, job, home and family are not incidental detail but a deliberate plainness: the more unremarkable he seems, the more easily we step into his shoes and carry his fears as our own. The point of beginning here is the contrast to come; an ordinary person forced into an extraordinary trial lets the reader believe that they, too, could rise to it.

**The concept:** The everyman is the reader stripped of every excuse for exceptionalism — an ordinary life so like our own that his choices become the ones we cannot pretend we would make differently. It asks whether courage and decency are the province of remarkable people, or an ordinary duty we quietly decline every day.

**Examples:** Jane, the poor, obscure, plain little governess whose deliberate ordinariness lets the reader inhabit her trial — Jane Eyre; George, the plain itinerant ranch hand whose unremarkable labouring life makes him the common man dispossessed by the Depression — Of Mice and Men; Pip, the ordinary blacksmith's boy whose plainness is the mask the reader wears as he is thrust into an extraordinary fortune — Great Expectations

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention settles on familiar, everyday detail. · Feel: Quiet empathy as the reader recognises themselves. · Think: Ordinary people can be drawn into great events. · Act: The reader leans in, ready to follow someone like them.

**How to analyse it:** Name the ordinariness precisely — the common job, home, name — then ask what the writer gains by making the reader recognise themselves in this figure before the story tests them. Read the Everyman as a mirror: their unremarkable start makes any courage or corruption feel available to us too, so the writer’s idea lands as ‘this could be you’. Track how far the plain beginning departs from the extraordinary end; in ‘A Christmas Carol’ Scrooge is drawn as an ordinary miser precisely so his transformation implicates every complacent reader.

**How to create it:** Build the Everyman by grounding them in the mundane specifics your reader shares — a routine, a small worry, a familiar setting — so the later trial feels earned rather than heroic-from-birth. Give them one ordinary want, then place an extraordinary demand across its path and let the gap generate the drama. Caution: ‘ordinary’ is not ‘blank’ — an Everyman with no texture reads as a placeholder, so grant one distinctive habit or flaw that makes the mirror specific.

**Kinds of Everyman** — The ordinary figure the reader stands beside — told many ways, each a different reason we see ourselves in them.

- **Kinds of everyman** — Why the reader identifies with this ordinary figure
  - **The Reluctant Witness** — An ordinary person drawn into events far larger than themselves. _(e.g. Nick observing the Buchanans' careless, glittering world — The Great Gatsby)_
  - **The Moral Anchor** — The plain, decent centre against which other characters are judged. _(e.g. Horatio, the steady friend left to tell the tale — Hamlet)_
  - **The Overlooked Hero** — An unremarkable figure who proves quietly, decisively brave. _(e.g. Samwise carrying Frodo when his strength gives out — The Lord of the Rings)_
  - **The Everyman in Extremis** — A normal person tested past their limits by extreme circumstance. _(e.g. Ralph struggling to hold order as the island unravels — Lord of the Flies)_
  - **The Representative Victim** — One ordinary life made to stand for the suffering of many. _(e.g. Tom Robinson, an innocent man crushed by a town's prejudice — To Kill a Mockingbird)_

### The Underdog `Ud`

The archetype of the disadvantaged, defined less by smallness than by the gap between what the protagonist is given and what they are up against. Low status, stacked odds and a thin sense of self-worth set the stakes high and the expectation low, so every step forward feels won rather than owed. We invest because we ache for the powerless to prevail; the suspense is whether the protagonist can find, in themselves, what circumstance has denied them.

**The concept:** The underdog answers the part of us that suspects our own worth has gone unweighed by a world already tilted towards those above us, so to cheer the outmatched is to stake a hope for ourselves. Yet it asks whether merit truly rises when the odds are honest, or whether we love the small only until they become the powerful.

**Examples:** Posner — the working-class, Jewish, openly gay scholarship boy, smallest and least sure of himself, set against the privilege and confidence of the Oxbridge race — The History Boys; Meena, the Punjabi immigrant girl growing up poor and othered in a hostile mining village, longing to belong in a world stacked against her — Anita and Me; the Sikh corner-shop newlywed, mocked and underestimated by the customers who jeer at his counter, quietly defiant of their low expectations — Singh Song

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention falls on the gap between the figure and the odds. · Feel: Compassion and admiration mingle with suspense. · Think: We weigh whether courage can outmatch disadvantage. · Act: The reader roots for the smaller, weaker side.

**How to analyse it:** Name the exact odds stacked against the figure — status, power, self-belief — and ask what the writer makes those odds mean about worth and effort. Read the Underdog as an argument that value is not fixed by starting position: the reader roots for them because their striving carries the writer’s idea that dignity is earned against resistance, not inherited. Weigh the internal deficit (low esteem) against the external one, since the deeper contest is often the character believing in themselves; Pip in ‘Great Expectations’ begins as a small, ashamed boy whose low self-worth is the true opponent his journey must overcome.

**How to create it:** Establish the deficit early and concretely — make the reader feel the smallness, the doubt, the disadvantage — then let progress come through effort and character rather than luck, so the win reads as deserved. Give the internal odds equal weight to the external ones; the most moving Underdog conquers their own doubt. Caution: do not make the victory total or effortless — an Underdog who wins easily forfeits the very struggle that made us care.

**Kinds of Underdog** — The disadvantaged figure we root for — told many ways, each a different disadvantage overcome or exposed.

- **Kinds of underdog** — The disadvantage faced, and whether it is overcome
  - **The Social Underdog** — Held down by class or poverty, striving upward against it. _(e.g. Pip, a blacksmith's boy reaching for gentility — Great Expectations)_
  - **The Overlooked Talent** — Dismissed by those around them until their worth is proven. _(e.g. Jane, plain and poor, whose mind outmatches her betters — Jane Eyre)_
  - **The Physical Underdog** — Outmatched in strength, winning through wit or sheer will. _(e.g. David going out to meet Goliath — the Bible)_
  - **The Doomed Underdog** — The disadvantage proves too great, and the world too cruel. _(e.g. Lennie, whose gentleness cannot survive — Of Mice and Men)_
  - **The Rising Many** — The powerless combine to challenge the powerful. _(e.g. the animals rising to overthrow Mr Jones — Animal Farm)_

### The Orphan `Or`

The archetype born of abandonment. Orphaned by death or cast out by family or society, this figure has lost the protection others take for granted, and that wound is the root of all the rest. The independence, wariness and hard pragmatism are defences built over loneliness; the fierce loyalty is a longing for the belonging they were denied. When the protagonist wears this mask, the story's true subject is the search for a home of one's own making.

**The concept:** The orphan names the part of us that has felt unclaimed — without a ready place, an inheritance, or anyone obliged to catch us — and asks what we make of ourselves once nothing is handed down. It presses the harder question of whether a self can be built from nothing, or whether we are forever shaped by what we were denied.

**Examples:** Pip, raised "by hand" with his parents dead, wary and self-made, hunting a belonging he was never given — Great Expectations; Jane, orphaned and cast out by the Reeds at Gateshead, fiercely self-reliant as she searches for a home of her own making — Jane Eyre; George and Lennie, family-less migrant workers clinging to the dream of "a little place" of their own, the belonging the road has denied them — Of Mice and Men

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention rests on self-reliance shadowed by vulnerability. · Feel: Empathy deepens into close identification. · Think: We reflect on how loss forges independence. · Act: The reader draws protectively closer.

**How to analyse it:** Name the severed tie — the missing family, guardian or belonging — and ask how that absence forges the pragmatism, cynicism and fierce loyalty the writer wants us to read. The Orphan’s independence is a wound turned into a stance: they trust the bonds they choose because the given ones failed, so their loyalty carries the writer’s idea about what family truly is. Track how the search for belonging drives the plot; Jane Eyre’s early orphaning teaches a self-reliance that later lets her refuse any belonging that costs her integrity.

**How to create it:** Build the Orphan by removing a foundational bond and letting the reader see how the character has hardened and adapted around the gap — wary on the surface, deeply loyal beneath. Drive their arc with a hunger for chosen belonging, and test it with an offer of the wrong kind. Caution: resist self-pity — the Orphan earns sympathy through resilience and pragmatism, not through dwelling on the loss.

**Kinds of Orphan** — The parentless figure seeking belonging — told many ways, each a different absence shaping the child.

- **Kinds of orphan** — How the lost parents shape the child left behind
  - **The Seeker of Belonging** — The orphan searches for a family or a place to call home. _(e.g. Oliver longing for warmth amid the workhouse — Oliver Twist)_
  - **The Self-Made Orphan** — Deprivation forges independence and moral steel. _(e.g. Jane refusing to bend even when quite alone in the world — Jane Eyre)_
  - **The Chosen One** — The orphan's lost origins conceal a special destiny. _(e.g. Harry, raised unloved, marked out for more — Harry Potter)_
  - **The Hardened Orphan** — Neglect curdles into resentment or cunning. _(e.g. Heathcliff, taken in and cast down, returning for revenge — Wuthering Heights)_
  - **The Haunted Orphan** — The absent parents shape the child as an open wound. _(e.g. Pip formed among graves of a family he never knew — Great Expectations)_

### The Lost Soul `Ls`

The archetype of the self-exiled. An emotional wound has driven this figure to the edge of the community and taught them to hide there, mistaking isolation for safety. Where the Orphan was cast out, the Lost Soul has withdrawn by choice, and that retreat is both the symptom of the wound and the thing keeping it open. As a mask the protagonist wears, it carries a warning and an invitation: they must risk being seen, or remain a stranger to the world and to themselves.

**The concept:** The lost soul touches the part of us that fears the quiet loss of meaning, belonging or faith — the anchors we assume will always hold until they slip. It asks what is left of a person once those moorings are gone, and whether anything can call them home.

**Examples:** The photographer who shuts himself away in his darkroom, set apart and unreachable from a public that will not be moved — "in his dark room he is finally alone" — War Photographer; The speaker who mourns a betrayed love in secret, withdrawn into a private grief he will not show — "In silence and tears" — When We Two Parted; The soldier driven to the edge by trauma, cut off from others and unable to wash away the guilt — "his bloody life in my bloody hands" — Remains

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention drifts to the figure’s distance and pain. · Feel: Unease softens into understanding. · Think: We consider what isolation does to a hurting person. · Act: The reader looks past the surface to seek them out.

**How to analyse it:** Name what wounded the figure and what they now hide from, then ask why the writer keeps them at the community’s edge — what the outsider lets us see that insiders cannot. The Lost Soul reads as the story’s conscience or its warning: their withdrawal exposes how the surrounding world treats difference, so the writer’s idea often turns on our misreading of them. Track the gap between how others see them and what they actually are; Boo Radley is feared as a monster until the outsider is revealed as the story’s gentlest protector, indicting the town that shunned him.

**How to create it:** Build the Lost Soul around a concealed wound and a chosen distance, letting behaviour hint at the hurt before any explanation arrives. Use the community’s misreading of them to say something about that community, and time the revelation of their true nature for maximum reversal. Caution: keep them a person, not a symbol of sadness — give the wound a specific cause so the withdrawal feels human rather than merely atmospheric.

**Kinds of Lost Soul** — The figure adrift from meaning or belonging — told many ways, each a different loss that unmoors them.

- **Kinds of lost soul** — The loss that leaves the figure adrift
  - **The Alienated Outsider** — Cut off from a society that will not admit them. _(e.g. the creature rejected everywhere for its appearance — Frankenstein)_
  - **The Disillusioned Wanderer** — A lost faith or purpose leaves them drifting. _(e.g. Holden adrift in a world he finds "phoney" — The Catcher in the Rye)_
  - **The Corrupted Soul** — Moral compromise gradually hollows them out. _(e.g. Macbeth, for whom life becomes "a tale told by an idiot" — Macbeth)_
  - **The Grieving Soul** — Loss freezes them in an unmoving past. _(e.g. Miss Havisham stopped at the hour of her betrayal — Great Expectations)_
  - **The Damned Soul** — Beyond redemption, and by their own choosing. _(e.g. Faustus, who cannot bring himself to repent — Doctor Faustus)_

### The Rebel `Rb`

The archetype of refusal, who looks at an unjust order and will not accept it. Outspoken and radical, the Rebel names the wrong aloud and draws others toward the change they privately wish for, so the reader feels their own suppressed desire for it voiced on the page. The danger of the mask is that defiance can curdle into destruction; when the protagonist wears it, the story tests whether their revolt serves justice or only their own anger.

**The concept:** The rebel speaks to the part of us that has ever privately doubted an authority we were told to obey without question, and thrilled at the thought of refusing. It asks whether an established order truly earns our allegiance, and what it costs a person to withhold it.

**Examples:** The animals' Rebellion, driving out Jones to overthrow an order they name as unjust — Animal Farm; The speaker who refuses the prejudiced label and turns it back on the reader — "Explain yuself / wha yu mean / when yu say half-caste" — Half-caste; The Duchess who would not rank his "nine-hundred-years-old name" above ordinary kindness, refusing to be owned — My Last Duchess

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention locks onto defiance aimed at injustice. · Feel: Admiration rises with an appetite for change. · Think: We question which rules deserve to be broken. · Act: The reader is stirred to challenge an unfair order.

**How to analyse it:** Name the injustice the figure names, and the crowd they move, then ask whether the writer frames the rebellion as principled or self-serving — the same archetype can be conscience or demagogue. Read the Rebel by their cause and their means: what they oppose reveals the writer’s idea about the order being challenged, and how they rally others reveals whether that challenge liberates or corrupts. In ‘Lord of the Flies’ Jack rebels against Ralph’s order not for justice but for power, so the archetype becomes the writer’s warning about how easily grievance is turned to tyranny.

**How to create it:** Build the Rebel by first making the injustice real and felt, so their defiance reads as earned rather than mere disruption, then give them the charisma to move others. Decide early whether they are a liberator or a would-be tyrant, and let their methods carry that answer. Caution: a Rebel with no genuine grievance is just a troublemaker — anchor the revolt in a wrong the reader can see.

**Kinds of Rebel** — The figure who defies the established order — told many ways, each a different order refused.

- **Kinds of rebel** — The order defied, and the cost of defiance
  - **The Political Rebel** — Rises against tyranny or an unjust regime. _(e.g. Winston resisting the Party's control of truth — Nineteen Eighty-Four)_
  - **The Moral Rebel** — Defies a corrupt norm for the sake of conscience. _(e.g. Atticus standing against his town to defend Tom — To Kill a Mockingbird)_
  - **The Romantic Rebel** — The brooding outsider who scorns all convention. _(e.g. Heathcliff raging against every rule that wronged him — Wuthering Heights)_
  - **The Doomed Rebel** — Defiance is crushed by the power it challenges. _(e.g. the lovers defying two feuding houses — Romeo and Juliet)_
  - **The Self-Serving Rebel** — Rebellion masks ambition, not principle. _(e.g. the pigs who overthrow one master only to become another — Animal Farm)_

### The Explorer `Ex`

The archetype of restlessness, driven by a hunger for the new and an instinct to push past every limit set in front of them. The Explorer rejects boundaries not for their own sake but because the unknown promises a fuller self than the safe and the familiar allow. As a mask the protagonist wears, this craving for experience is what carries them across the threshold into the trial. Its shadow is the temptation to keep moving rather than ever arrive, so the story asks what they are truly seeking.

**The concept:** The explorer is the part of us that chafes at the familiar and needs to step beyond it — every frontier crossed on the page answers our own restlessness to know the world and, in leaving home, to find out who we are. Yet it also asks whether some limits exist for good reason, and what is lost when nothing is left unknown.

**Examples:** Jane, restless within Thornfield's walls and hungering for "a power of vision which might overpass that limit" of the life laid out for her — Jane Eyre; the boys driven by a hunger for knowledge beyond the prescribed syllabus, reaching past the limits the exam sets for them — The History Boys

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention follows the figure past the edge of the known. · Feel: Curiosity quickens into the thrill of the unknown. · Think: We weigh the worth of stepping beyond comfort. · Act: The reader feels invited to venture past their limits.

**How to analyse it:** Name what boundary the figure refuses and what they seek beyond it, then ask what the writer uses their restlessness to reveal about the ordinary world left behind. The Explorer’s hunger for the new drives the reader across a threshold, and what they find there — wonder or danger — carries the writer’s idea about curiosity’s rewards and costs. Track what the journey teaches them about home; Lucy Pevensie’s readiness to step through into Narnia opens a world the cautious cannot reach, so her openness itself becomes the story’s value.

**How to create it:** Build the Explorer by giving them a visible impatience with limits — a question, an itch, a closed door they cannot leave shut — and let that trait, not chance, launch the plot. Use their perspective to make the new world feel freshly seen. Caution: pair the appetite with a cost, or the Explorer becomes a tour guide — what they gain in experience should risk something they hold at home.

**Kinds of Explorer** — The figure who ventures beyond the known — told many ways, each a different frontier crossed.

- **Kinds of explorer** — The frontier crossed, and what the crossing costs
  - **The Physical Explorer** — Ventures into uncharted seas and unknown lands. _(e.g. Odysseus driven across unknown waters toward home — The Odyssey)_
  - **The Explorer of Knowledge** — Presses past the limits of what may safely be known. _(e.g. Victor breaching the boundary between life and death — Frankenstein)_
  - **The Inner Explorer** — The frontier is the self and its own conscience. _(e.g. Jane mapping her own principles across each trial — Jane Eyre)_
  - **The Escapist Explorer** — Flees the ordinary world for a stranger one. _(e.g. the children stepping through the wardrobe into Narnia — The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe)_
  - **The Doomed Explorer** — The crossing destroys the one who dares it. _(e.g. Ahab hunting his whale to the very last — Moby-Dick)_

### The Mentor `Mr`

The archetype of inherited wisdom. The Mentor has already walked the road the protagonist now faces, and so can give the tools, training and moral compass the hero still lacks. A true Mentor does not hand over answers; they challenge the protagonist to find their own strength. Crucially, the Mentor must usually depart — killed, gone or stepped back — forcing the protagonist to stand alone, so the loss is itself the test that proves they have grown. As a mask, it marks the moment the protagonist becomes the one who guides others.

**The concept:** The mentor answers the part of us that longs for someone wiser to make out the path we cannot yet see, and to be trusted with it. It asks what one generation owes the next in hard-won wisdom, and what is broken in us when that trust is betrayed.

**Examples:** Hector teaching the boys to love literature for its own sake rather than for the exam, then forced out and killed so they must go on without him — The History Boys; Magwitch as the hidden benefactor who shapes Pip's prospects then is taken from him, forcing Pip to stand alone — Great Expectations; Friar Lawrence counselling Romeo and supplying the plan and the potion, whose absence at the tomb leaves the lovers to act alone and die — Romeo and Juliet

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention turns to wisdom offered to a younger figure. · Feel: Respect and reassurance steady the reader. · Think: We absorb the solution the figure demonstrates. · Act: The reader is moved to heed counsel and pass it on.

**How to analyse it:** Name what the Mentor knows that the protagonist does not, and ask why the writer gives that wisdom to a separate figure rather than the hero themselves — usually to mark a lesson the protagonist must grow to hold alone. Read the Mentor by their function in the protagonist’s journey: they supply guidance, faith and often a departure, so their removal forces the hero to internalise what was taught. Track the handover of belief; a Mentor who keeps faith in the good, then steps back, lets the writer say that wisdom must finally be carried, not borrowed.

**How to create it:** Deploy the Mentor to equip the protagonist with a truth or tool, then plan their absence — death, betrayal or exit — so the hero must apply the lesson without them. Keep the guidance partial; the Mentor points, the protagonist must still walk. Caution: avoid the all-knowing oracle who solves the plot — a Mentor who could win it themselves steals the protagonist’s growth, so give them a limit or a blind spot.

**Kinds of Mentor** — The guide who shapes the hero — told many ways, each a different kind of wisdom offered.

- **Kinds of mentor** — The kind of guidance offered, and its motive
  - **The Sage** — Imparts wisdom and sets the hero on their path. _(e.g. Gandalf guiding a reluctant company — The Lord of the Rings)_
  - **The Protector** — Shields and trains the hero until they can stand alone. _(e.g. Atticus teaching his children to see through others' eyes — To Kill a Mockingbird)_
  - **The Fallen Mentor** — Once wise, now compromised, and teaching harm. _(e.g. Miss Havisham schooling Estella to break men's hearts — Great Expectations)_
  - **The False Mentor** — Guidance that serves the guide, not the hero. _(e.g. Fagin training children to thieve for his profit — Oliver Twist)_
  - **The Sacrificial Mentor** — Dies so that the hero may go on. _(e.g. Dumbledore falling so Harry can face what comes — Harry Potter)_

### The Trickster `Tk`

The archetype of disruption. The Trickster is a cunning, boundary-crossing agent of chaos who is bored by order and delights in turning a settled world upside down. Their wit is a weapon: by humbling the arrogant and exposing hypocrisy, they force the comfortable out of their certainties. They rarely grow themselves; instead they instigate growth in others, cracking open a stable world so its truths spill out and the reader sees it afresh. As a mask, the Trickster lets the protagonist break a rule the story needs broken — or, worn by another, becomes the catalyst that knocks the protagonist off their fixed path.

**The concept:** The trickster gives voice to the part of us that sees through the pompous and the self-important yet holds its tongue, and delights when someone finally will not. It asks who really holds authority, and whether wit can speak truths that rank and office never dare.

**Examples:** Puck delighting in the chaos of misapplied love-juice — "Lord, what fools these mortals be!" — A Midsummer Night's Dream; Feste the fool, who crosses every household and exposes folly while staying outside the chaos he stirs — Twelfth Night

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention darts to mischief that upends the order. · Feel: Amusement breaks the tension with comic relief. · Think: We gain a fresh, sideways angle on the situation. · Act: The reader is prompted to question who holds power.

**How to analyse it:** Name whose inflated ego the Trickster deflates and how — wit, mischief, disruption — then ask what order the writer lets them expose by breaking it. The Trickster reads as the story’s licensed truth-teller: their chaos punctures pretension, so their pranks carry the writer’s critique of the pompous or corrupt. Track who is humbled and what that humbling reveals; in the Fool tradition, mockery says what earnest speech cannot, and even a self-serving Trickster like Fagin exposes the hypocrisies of the respectable world that produced him.

**How to create it:** Deploy the Trickster to disrupt a stiff or corrupt order, using cunning rather than force, and aim the mischief at a target whose comeuppance the reader will relish. Let them speak truths the serious characters cannot. Caution: keep the chaos purposeful — a Trickster who disrupts for no end reads as noise, so tie each trick to an ego that deserves puncturing or a truth that needs airing.

**Kinds of Trickster** — The figure who works by wit and mischief — told many ways, each a different use of cunning.

- **Kinds of trickster** — How the cunning is used, and to what end
  - **The Comic Trickster** — Mischief for delight and gleeful disruption. _(e.g. Puck confounding the lovers in the wood — A Midsummer Night's Dream)_
  - **The Wise Fool** — Folly that alone dares speak the hidden truth. _(e.g. the Fool telling Lear what no courtier will — King Lear)_
  - **The Malicious Trickster** — Cunning bent deliberately to harm. _(e.g. Iago poisoning Othello's mind by insinuation — Othello)_
  - **The Survivor** — Wit as the weapon of the powerless. _(e.g. the small creature who outwits stronger foes in folk tale — Brer Rabbit)_
  - **The Rogue with a Heart** — A charming deceiver who proves loyal underneath. _(e.g. the Artful Dodger, pickpocket yet comrade — Oliver Twist)_

### The Shadow `Sd`

The archetype of the repressed self. The Shadow embodies everything the protagonist hides, denies or fears in themselves — and the more it is denied, the more power it gathers. It works as a dark mirror, often sharing the protagonist's own gifts but turning them to destructive ends, so the conflict is really a confrontation with the self. It may appear as an external villain or rival, yet its true function is to drag the protagonist's buried darkness into the light. The story's deepest test is the choice to either destroy the Shadow, losing part of oneself, or to face and integrate it and become whole.

**The concept:** The shadow confronts us with the destructive impulses we recognise in ourselves and would rather disown, made visible in another. It asks whether the evil we set ourselves against is ever wholly separate from the evil we carry.

**Examples:** Hyde as the violence Jekyll represses and disowns, sharing his body yet enacting the cruelty Jekyll's respectability denies — Dr Jekyll and Mr Hyde; the murdered looter who returns to haunt the soldier, the killing he cannot own resurfacing as the self he must finally face — Remains; the calm, tender voice masking the possessive, murderous self the speaker will not admit, his love turned to strangling — Porphyria's Lover

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention is pulled towards menace and threat. · Feel: Fear and dread tighten as danger draws near. · Think: We confront the fears the figure embodies. · Act: The reader braces for the good to be tested.

**How to analyse it:** Name what the Shadow threatens and, crucially, what part of the protagonist it externalises — the archetype often embodies the hero’s own repressed capacity for harm. Read the Shadow as the measure of the good it opposes: its power, method and appeal define what the protagonist must resist or overcome, so it carries the writer’s idea about the darkness the story confronts. Track the mirror between them; Hyde is not a separate villain but Jekyll’s own severed appetite, so the Shadow reveals the writer’s warning that the monster is grown within, not met without.

**How to create it:** Build the Shadow to embody exactly what your protagonist fears, represses or could become, so the conflict reads as internal as well as external. Give it real power and, ideally, a distorted logic that tempts, not just terrifies. Caution: resist pure, motiveless evil — a Shadow that mirrors a flaw in the hero cuts far deeper than a monster who is simply bad, and keeps the contest morally alive.

**Kinds of Shadow** — The dark mirror of the hero — told many ways, each a different darkness it reflects.

- **Kinds of shadow** — Where the darkness sits in relation to the hero
  - **The Villain** — The external antagonist embodying evil to be defeated. _(e.g. Sauron, malice given a single will — The Lord of the Rings)_
  - **The Doppelganger** — A double who externalises the hero's darker half. _(e.g. Hyde, the shape Jekyll's appetites finally take — Jekyll and Hyde)_
  - **The Inner Shadow** — The darkness lives within the protagonist themselves. _(e.g. the savagery the boys uncover in their own hearts — Lord of the Flies)_
  - **The Tempter** — The shadow that lures the hero toward the fall. _(e.g. the witches feeding Macbeth's waiting ambition — Macbeth)_
  - **The Sympathetic Shadow** — A dark mirror the reader is made to pity. _(e.g. the creature, monstrous yet deeply wronged — Frankenstein)_

### The Hero `He`

The archetype defined not by perfection but by the journey. The Hero is called from an ordinary world into the unknown and, because they begin flawed or incomplete, must confront their own weakness in order to overcome the obstacle outside them. Their courage is real only because it costs something: the willingness to sacrifice safety, name or life for a cause greater than themselves. As a mask, the Hero IS the protagonist at full stretch, and the shift into it from earlier masks is the visible evidence of the change the whole narrative exists to earn.

**The concept:** The hero answers the part of us that wonders who we would prove to be when everything is at stake — the braver self we hope is waiting beneath the fear. Each kind of hero asks a different question about what courage really is, and whom a society permits to embody it.

**Examples:** Jane walking out into the night with nothing rather than betray her own integrity, courage paid for in everything she loses — Jane Eyre; Scrooge, summoned from his closed, money-bound life by the spirits, confronting the coldness in himself and vowing 'I will honour Christmas in my heart, and try to keep it all the year' — A Christmas Carol; Pip, raised a common forge-boy and ashamed of his origins, leaving his ordinary world for London in pursuit of becoming a gentleman, only to confront his own snobbery and ingratitude before he can return to value Joe's love, courage measured by the humbling of his own pride — Great Expectations

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention rises to courage and willing sacrifice. · Feel: Admiration swells into a thrill of aspiration. · Think: We consider what we ourselves might dare. · Act: The reader is moved to act bravely.

**How to analyse it:** Name what the Hero risks and for whom, then ask whether the writer tests their courage against fear, temptation or self-interest — the sacrifice is where the idea lives. Read the Hero by their want against their need: ambition for the good drives them, but the story usually asks them to surrender something personal for it, and that cost defines the value the writer upholds. Track the moment of chosen sacrifice; a Hero proves the writer’s idea not by winning but by what they are willing to give up to win.

**How to create it:** Build the Hero around a cause worth risking themselves for, then design the trial so victory demands a real sacrifice — comfort, safety, a relationship, the self. Let courage be a choice made against genuine fear, not a default setting. Caution: avoid the flawless champion — an invulnerable Hero has nothing to overcome, so grant a fear or weakness their sacrifice must cost them.

**Kinds of Hero** — The central figure who acts and is tested — told many ways, each a different kind of heroism.

- **Kinds of hero** — The kind of heroism the figure embodies
  - **The Classical Hero** — Noble, brave and larger than ordinary life. _(e.g. Beowulf facing monster after monster — Beowulf)_
  - **The Everyman Hero** — An ordinary person who rises to an extraordinary moment. _(e.g. Samwise's plain courage carrying the quest — The Lord of the Rings)_
  - **The Anti-Hero** — Flawed or unheroic, yet the one we follow. _(e.g. Holden, prickly and adrift, still ours to sympathise with — The Catcher in the Rye)_
  - **The Tragic Hero** — Greatness undone by a single error in action. _(e.g. Macbeth, a valiant soldier turned tyrant — Macbeth)_
  - **The Byronic Hero** — Brooding, transgressive and magnetic. _(e.g. Heathcliff, cruel yet compelling — Wuthering Heights)_

### Hero’s Journey `Hj`

The universal pattern every other plot is a variation of: the external map of an internal change. The protagonist begins in a false identity, asleep to who they truly are, until a call forces them across a threshold into a testing world. Trials, a mentor and a lowest point strip away the false self so the true self can emerge. The journey is won not when the goal is reached but when the protagonist is transformed; the closing image stands as the opposite of the opening, proving the change is real.

**The concept:** The hero's road outward is the road every life takes away from the sheltered self — we follow the trials because becoming who we are asks that we leave behind the child we were, and no one is spared the summons. Yet it asks what a person must surrender for that growth, and whether the self that returns can ever be fully at home again.

**Examples:** Jane leaving Gateshead and Thornfield through trial after trial to return self-possessed and equal — Jane Eyre; Bilbo pulled from his hobbit-hole into the road, tested, and returned changed beyond the comfort he began in — The Hobbit; Meena drawn out of her idolised Tollington childhood through the testing of who she pretends to be, leaving for grammar school as a self she has chosen — Anita and Me

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention tracks the protagonist from ordinary world into trial. · Feel: Excitement, fear and anticipation build toward triumph. · Think: We reflect on transformation and self-discovery. · Act: The reader feels called to their own adventure.

**How to analyse it:** Trace the protagonist across the threshold: call, refusal, trials, the deepest ordeal, and the return — then ask what inner change the outer journey exists to produce. Read the Hero’s Journey as want versus need: the quest they think they are on masks the transformation they actually undergo, so the writer’s idea lives in what they learn, not merely what they achieve. Weigh the person who returns against the one who left; the whole shape is a machine for change, and the treasure is usually the changed self brought home.

**How to create it:** Plan the arc as a departure, an ordeal that forces growth, and a return where the protagonist applies what they learned, keeping the inner change ahead of the outer events. Anchor each stage to their need, not just the plot’s obstacles. Caution: the beats are a scaffold, not a checklist — hitting every stage mechanically produces a formula, so let a fresh idea bend the structure rather than the structure dictate the idea.

**Kinds of Hero's Journey** — One departure-and-return told through many callings — each a different shape the adventure takes and what the hero carries home.

- **Kinds of journey** — Why the hero leaves the known world, and what they bring back
  - **The Reluctant Hero** — The hero refuses the call, then rises to meet it. _(e.g. Bilbo pulled from his comfortable hole into a dangerous world — The Hobbit)_
  - **The Coming-of-Power** — An ordinary figure discovers a capacity they never suspected. _(e.g. Harry learning he is a wizard and claiming that inheritance — Harry Potter)_
  - **The Inward Journey** — The road is psychological; the treasure is self-knowledge. _(e.g. Jane's search for belonging and moral autonomy across each house she inhabits — Jane Eyre)_
  - **The Fallen Hero** — The quest curdles because the hero's own flaw poisons it. _(e.g. Victor's hunger for forbidden knowledge destroying all he loves — Frankenstein)_
  - **The Return with Wisdom** — The hero comes home altered, to remake the world they left. _(e.g. Scout carrying her father's lesson in empathy back into her town — To Kill a Mockingbird)_

### Coming of Age `Cg`

The Hero's Journey told as growing up: the change is from innocence to experience. The protagonist starts in youthful naivety, unaware of their own potential and shielded from the world's hard truths, and a formative ordeal forces them to shed that childhood self. Maturity is the prize, but it is bought with a loss of innocence, which is why the form carries nostalgia as well as hope. The reader watches a self being built under pressure, and recognises their own becoming in it.

**The concept:** The young protagonist stands at the threshold every reader has crossed, the moment a comforting illusion first gave way to the world as it truly is — we recognise the passage because none of us reached ourselves without paying its price. It asks what truth is worth the innocence it costs, and whether the knowing leaves us the richer or the poorer for it.

**Examples:** Mickey and Eddie growing from playing children into divided men as class hardens around them — Blood Brothers; Scout learning, through the trial and Boo Radley, to stand in another's shoes and lose her childhood certainties — To Kill a Mockingbird; the speaker scaling his grandfather's body as a child's reverent ascent toward understanding an elder — Climbing My Grandfather

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention follows innocence as it meets experience. · Feel: Empathy and nostalgia warm into hope. · Think: We weigh the cost and gift of growing up. · Act: The reader reflects on their own growth.

**How to analyse it:** Name the two inner states the protagonist moves between — innocence to maturity, ignorance to knowledge, illusion to disillusion — and ask what specific experience forces the shift. Read Coming of Age by the price of the knowledge gained: the protagonist trades one way of seeing for another, and the writer’s idea sits in what is lost as much as what is understood. Track the moment the old view becomes untenable; Scout’s dawning grasp of injustice in ‘To Kill a Mockingbird’ marks the exact point innocence gives way to moral sight, and cannot be undone.

**How to create it:** Choose the two states clearly — where the protagonist begins and where they must end — then build one or more formative experiences that make the old state impossible to keep. Let the change cost something, so growth reads as trade, not upgrade. Caution: avoid the tidy moral lesson delivered from outside — the shift must be forced by lived experience, not announced, or the transformation feels imposed rather than earned.

**Archetypal Journeys** — The coming-of-age story is one arc told through many transformations — each a shift from one inner state to its opposite.

- **Archetypal journeys** — The before-and-after transformation the protagonist undergoes
  - **Innocence to Experience** — The protagonist loses a childhood certainty and meets the world’s complexity for the first time. _(e.g. Scout learning that decent people can still hold indecent prejudices — To Kill a Mockingbird)_
  - **Ignorance to Knowledge** — The protagonist awakens to a truth — social, moral or personal — they were previously blind to. _(e.g. Sheila recognising her family’s complicity and her own social conscience — An Inspector Calls)_
  - **Selfishness to Responsibility** — The protagonist stops acting only for themselves and accepts a duty to others. _(e.g. Eric confronting the consequences of his conduct and owning his share of the blame — An Inspector Calls)_
  - **Illusion to Disillusionment** — The protagonist’s idealised expectations meet reality, and false values fall away. _(e.g. Pip discovering that wealth and gentility are hollow measures of worth — Great Expectations)_
  - **Dependence to Independence** — The protagonist moves from relying on others to self-possession and moral autonomy. _(e.g. Jane refusing to compromise her integrity, choosing self-respect over security — Jane Eyre)_
  - **Conformity to Self-identity** — The protagonist breaks from what is expected of them to claim their own values. _(e.g. Holden resisting the "phoniness" he sees in the adult world as he searches for who he is — The Catcher in the Rye)_

### Overcoming the Monster `Om`

The Hero's Journey shaped as a confrontation with an overwhelming evil that casts its shadow over a whole community. The monster looks unbeatable and the protagonist tiny and alone, because the outer battle mirrors an inner one: defeating the monster is also defeating the old, frightened self that believed it could not. Victory restores the threatened community and liberates it from fear. The reader feels tension resolve into triumph, learning that courage is survivable and evil is not absolute.

**The concept:** The monster gathers into a single shape the dread a whole people carries, so that facing it on the page rehearses the courage we each need to turn and stand against our own fear. Yet it asks whether we can meet that darkness and defeat it without being made monstrous by the fight.

**Examples:** Beowulf descending into the mere to kill Grendel, the man-eating evil that has emptied Heorot of joy for twelve years — Beowulf; Utterson hunting down the predatory Hyde whose violence is terrorising the streets of London, only to find the monster is the respectable Jekyll himself — Jekyll and Hyde; the boys' war against 'the beast' on the island, an external monster that turns out to be the savagery rising inside them — Lord of the Flies

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention fixes on the threat and the courage to meet it. · Feel: Tension and fear give way to triumph. · Think: We consider good prevailing over evil. · Act: The reader is stirred to stand firm.

**How to analyse it:** Name the monster and what it represents — a fear, an injustice, a force — then ask what courage the protagonist must find in themselves to meet it. Read Overcoming the Monster by the internal deficit the threat exposes: the true battle is often the hero becoming capable of the fight, so the writer’s idea concerns the courage the ordeal demands. Track the turn from fear to resolve; the monster’s scale measures the growth required, and its defeat confirms what the writer holds the protagonist — and the reader — must summon.

**How to create it:** Build the monster to embody a threat that matters thematically, then make the protagonist initially unequal to it, so the plot becomes their gathering of the courage or skill to prevail. Let the final confrontation demand the very quality they lacked at the start. Caution: a monster with no meaning is just a hazard — tie it to a fear or wrong the reader recognises, or the victory feels hollow.

**Kinds of Monster** — One confrontation told through many monsters — each the shape a threat takes when it must be faced.

- **Kinds of monster** — The form the threat takes, and how the protagonist must meet it
  - **The External Beast** — A literal predator embodying primal danger, to be hunted or fled. _(e.g. the vampire preying on a rational modern world — Dracula)_
  - **The Human Tyrant** — A person whose cruelty and power must be broken to restore order. _(e.g. the tyrant Macduff rises to overthrow — Macbeth)_
  - **The Inner Demon** — The monster is within: appetite, ambition or a divided self. _(e.g. the second self Jekyll frees and can no longer cage — Jekyll and Hyde)_
  - **The Societal Monster** — An unjust system or comfortable complacency the protagonist confronts. _(e.g. the family's self-interest the Inspector indicts — An Inspector Calls)_
  - **The Monster We Become** — The protagonist turns into the very thing they first feared or fought. _(e.g. the boys who hunt a beast and descend into savagery themselves — Lord of the Flies)_

### Rags to Riches `Rr`

The Hero's Journey told as a rise from a lowly, oppressed beginning, where dark figures belittle the protagonist and keep them small. The visible rise to wealth or status is the outer story; the real one is the protagonist growing into their true worth from the inside. This is why riches sought selfishly ring hollow and can collapse into failure, while riches earned through virtue and self-mastery endure. The reader feels hope and vindication: the deserving are seen at last.

**The concept:** The rise from nothing answers our hope that patience and decency might one day be repaid — the same faith that lets any of us keep working towards a life we cannot yet see. It asks what worth truly is, whether it lies in the fortune finally won or in the character that holds firm beneath it.

**Examples:** Pip the blacksmith's boy raised to a gentleman by secret money, whose hollow new wealth collapses until he learns his true worth — Great Expectations; the working-class state-school boys drilled out of obscurity to seize the Oxbridge places they were never meant to reach — The History Boys; the orphaned dependent humiliated by the Reeds and starved at Lowood, who declares "I am no bird; and no net ensnares me: I am a free human being with an independent will," and rises through an inherited fortune to marry Rochester as an equal — Jane Eyre

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention rests on the climb toward reward. · Feel: Hope and empathy lift into joy. · Think: We reflect on virtue and perseverance rewarded. · Act: The reader is encouraged to persist.

**How to analyse it:** Name the humble or oppressed start and the virtue that drives the rise, then ask whether the writer treats the riches as reward or as a test of the character who gains them. Read Rags to Riches by what the ascent reveals about worth: perseverance and integrity carry the protagonist upward, so the writer’s idea concerns what truly ennobles a person. Watch whether wealth completes or corrupts them; Pip’s rise in ‘Great Expectations’ turns the pattern critical, since his new riches distance him from the goodness that made him worthy, questioning the reward itself.

**How to create it:** Plot the rise through perseverance and virtue so the elevation reads as earned, and decide whether the riches vindicate the protagonist or expose them. Use the contrast between the humble start and the elevated end to carry your idea about worth. Caution: beware the fairy-tale shortcut where luck alone lifts them — the ascent must be driven by character, or it says nothing about what deserves reward.

**Kinds of Rise** — One ascent told many ways — each a different climb, and a different verdict on what the rise costs.

- **Kinds of rise** — How the fortune is won, and what the winning reveals
  - **The Earned Rise** — Integrity and merit rewarded with a fortune the protagonist deserves. _(e.g. Jane returning to Rochester on her own terms, an equal at last — Jane Eyre)_
  - **The Hollow Rise** — Wealth arrives but proves an empty measure of worth. _(e.g. Pip discovering that gentility has made him ashamed of better people — Great Expectations)_
  - **The Corrupting Rise** — The climb is built on illusion or crime and rots what it touches. _(e.g. Gatsby's self-made fortune, and the dream it can never buy — The Great Gatsby)_
  - **The Dream Deferred** — The rise is longed for but stays forever out of reach. _(e.g. the farm George and Lennie will never own — Of Mice and Men)_
  - **The Rise and Fall** — Fortune is seized and then lost, undone by the means that won it. _(e.g. the crown Macbeth takes that destroys him — Macbeth)_

### Rebirth / Redemption `Rd`

The Hero's Journey of a soul in a living death: a protagonist begins cold, harmful and cut off from others, often a Lost Soul who pretends not to care because he believes he is past saving. A warning or second chance forces him to confront the wound that hardened him, and the dark force he must defeat is his own old self. Redemption arrives when he chooses humanity over isolation and rejoins the community he had wronged. The reader feels relief and hope: no one is beyond change.

**The concept:** The second chance draws us because everyone carries some part of themselves gone cold, and longs to believe even a hardened heart can still be thawed and begun again. It asks what it takes to melt that frozen self, and whether the chance arrives while there is still time to seize it.

**Examples:** Scrooge, the cold solitary miser warned by the spirits, confronting the boy he was and waking remade to rejoin the community he had despised — A Christmas Carol; the proud, contemptuous Mr Darcy, shamed by Elizabeth's refusal into facing his own arrogance and remaking himself into the man who quietly saves the Bennets — Pride and Prejudice; the deceiving, hardened Rochester, his pride burned and his sight lost in the fire, brought low and morally remade before he is allowed to rejoin Jane — Jane Eyre

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention turns to the flaw and the chance to set it right. · Feel: Hope and relief soften into empathy. · Think: We consider guilt, atonement and forgiveness. · Act: The reader believes second chances are possible.

**How to analyse it:** Name the flaw the protagonist must repent, the second chance offered, and what forces them to see themselves clearly — the turn is the whole point. Read Rebirth or Redemption by the confrontation that cracks the old self: the writer engineers a reckoning that makes change both possible and necessary, so the idea concerns the human capacity to become better. Track the exact hinge of transformation; Scrooge is granted a supernatural review of his life in ‘A Christmas Carol’ so that fear and grief force self-recognition, and the reformed man proves the writer’s faith that no one is beyond change.

**How to create it:** Build the arc by establishing the flaw vividly, then engineering a reckoning — a loss, a vision, a consequence — that forces genuine self-recognition before change. Make the transformation cost effort and follow-through, not just a change of heart. Caution: guard against the instant conversion — redemption that arrives without a hard confrontation reads as unearned, so let the protagonist truly face what they were.

**Kinds of Rebirth** — One transformation told many ways — each a different darkness the protagonist is brought back from.

- **Kinds of rebirth** — The stasis that is broken, and what breaks it
  - **The Moral Redemption** — A hardened heart is softened and the self remade. _(e.g. Scrooge waking changed on Christmas morning — A Christmas Carol)_
  - **The Awakening from Illusion** — A false life is shed for a truer one. _(e.g. Nora walking out of her doll's house into selfhood — A Doll's House)_
  - **The Thaw** — Long-frozen feeling is coaxed back to life. _(e.g. the neglected children brought to life by a hidden garden — The Secret Garden)_
  - **The Rescue from Without** — An outside force breaks the spell of stasis. _(e.g. the curse lifted only when the Beast is loved — Beauty and the Beast)_
  - **The Failed Rebirth** — The chance to change comes too late, or is refused. _(e.g. Miss Havisham frozen in the moment of her betrayal — Great Expectations)_

### The Quest `Qu`

The Hero's Journey organised around a single, named goal, a treasure or kingdom that pulls the protagonist and their companions out across hostile terrain. The external object gives the story its drive, yet it is partly a façade: the journey towards it is what tests and remakes the protagonist within. The prize is won, but the deeper prize is the self discovered in the pursuit of it. The reader feels anticipation build to fulfilment, and learns that the true reward of a search is the searcher it produces.

**The concept:** The quest gives shapeless longing a destination, the single goal towards which a scattered life can gather its effort and hope — for we all need something to walk towards. Yet the prize sought is seldom the prize found, and it asks what the road itself teaches that arrival alone never could.

**Examples:** Odysseus driving his crew across a hostile sea toward the single goal of home in Ithaca, remade by every trial on the way — The Odyssey; the fellowship's perilous march to cast the One Ring into Mount Doom, the journey costing and proving each of them — The Lord of the Rings; Bilbo and the dwarves' long road to reclaim the treasure under the Lonely Mountain, the timid hobbit becoming brave in the pursuit — The Hobbit

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention is drawn forward, fixed on the distant goal. · Feel: Determination and wonder sustain the journey. · Think: The path reveals more than the prize. · Act: The reader is spurred to pursue a goal of their own.

**How to analyse it:** Name the explicit goal and the road to it, then ask what the protagonist discovers about themselves that the goal alone could not teach. Read the Quest by the gap between the object sought and the self found: the destination structures the plot, but the writer’s idea usually lives in the journey’s trials and companions. Track how the road changes the traveller; in ‘The Lord of the Rings’ the goal is to destroy the Ring, but the quest’s true yield is what endurance, fellowship and temptation reveal in those who carry it.

**How to create it:** Give the Quest a clear, concrete goal to pull the plot forward, then load the journey with trials and choices that reveal and reshape the protagonist along the way. Let the ending answer an inner question, not only the outer one. Caution: do not let the goal become a mere errand — if the road teaches the traveller nothing, the quest is travel, not story.

**Kinds of Quest** — One journey toward a goal told many ways — each a different thing sought, and a different truth found on the way.

- **Kinds of quest** — What is sought, and what the seeking costs
  - **The Object Quest** — A definite prize drives the whole journey. _(e.g. the ring that must be carried to the fire and destroyed — The Lord of the Rings)_
  - **The Homecoming** — The goal is return, not acquisition. _(e.g. Odysseus battling ten years to reach Ithaca — The Odyssey)_
  - **The Quest for Knowledge** — The goal is a truth, and the truth proves dangerous. _(e.g. Faustus trading his soul for forbidden learning — Doctor Faustus)_
  - **The Moral Quest** — The true goal is justice or redress. _(e.g. the defence of Tom Robinson against a town's prejudice — To Kill a Mockingbird)_
  - **The Doomed Quest** — The object is unreachable, or destroys the one who seeks it. _(e.g. Ahab's obsessive hunt for the white whale — Moby-Dick)_

### Tragedy (plot) `Ty`

The Hero's Journey run in reverse: the same stages of trial and temptation, but the protagonist never lowers the false self and so descends instead of rising. The engine is not a 'fatal flaw' but hamartia, an error in action — a choice the protagonist makes, usually driven by hubris or an unhealed wound. Each wrong decision tightens the chain of consequence until recognition comes too late to save them, and the fall destroys them. Its purpose is catharsis and warning: the downfall indicts the pride, or the society, that made the error possible, so the community either learns or is condemned to repeat it.

**The concept:** A downfall is never one person's doing alone, for it lays bare the world whose values made the error in action feel like the only course — and each of us acts inside just such a world, half-blind to what it asks of us. Our pity for the fallen becomes a judgement on that order, and it asks how far the same values still bind our own hands.

**Examples:** Victor's hubristic act of creation, then his refusal to take responsibility for the creature, setting in motion a chain of deaths he recognises he caused only when everyone he loves is gone — Frankenstein; Faustus signing away his soul for power and clinging to pride until the bargain drags him down past saving — Doctor Faustus; Macbeth choosing to murder Duncan to seize the crown, then committing further killings to secure it, each fresh decision tightening the trap until the throne he chose to take destroys him — Macbeth

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention narrows to the misjudgement steering the fall. · Feel: Pity and dread deepen into catharsis. · Think: We weigh how a single decision can undo greatness. · Act: The reader is cautioned to examine their own choices.

**How to analyse it:** Name the protagonist’s high standing and the single hamartia — an error in action, not an inborn ‘fatal flaw’ — that sets the fall in motion, then trace the chain of consequence it cannot recall. Read Tragedy by the timing of recognition: the protagonist grasps the truth of what they have done too late to act on it, so the writer’s idea concerns the irreversibility of a chosen misstep. Track want against the ruin it brings; Macbeth’s murder of Duncan is the error in action — a deed, not a defect — that unleashes a consequence he cannot stop and recognises only when nothing can be undone.

**How to create it:** Build the tragedy by raising the protagonist high, then having them commit a specific misjudged action that begins an unstoppable chain, so the fall follows from a deed rather than a stated flaw. Delay their full recognition until it is too late to act. Caution: never reduce the cause to a ‘fatal flaw’ label — a tragedy turns on a chosen error, so dramatise the decisive action and let its consequences, not a diagnosis, carry the fall.

**Kinds of Tragedy** — One fall told many ways — each a different engine that drives a protagonist to ruin.

- **Kinds of tragedy** — The force that drives the protagonist down
  - **The Tragedy of Ambition** — The protagonist is destroyed by their own reaching. _(e.g. the murders by which Macbeth climbs to a crown that damns him — Macbeth)_
  - **The Fatal Error (hamartia)** — A single error in action unravels everything. _(e.g. Othello's misplaced trust curdling into fatal jealousy — Othello)_
  - **The Tragedy of Fate** — Forces beyond the protagonist crush a course already doomed. _(e.g. the "star-crossed" lovers marked from the opening lines — Romeo and Juliet)_
  - **The Tragedy of Pride (hubris)** — Arrogance and overreach invite the fall. _(e.g. Victor presuming to master the limits set on man — Frankenstein)_
  - **The Tragedy of Circumstance** — Decent people are broken by an indifferent world. _(e.g. George forced to end Lennie's dream with his own hand — Of Mice and Men)_

**Related:** Tragedy

### Voyage and Return `Vr`

The pattern of the threshold crossed and recrossed. A restless or unfulfilled protagonist is pulled out of the ordinary world into a strange one that first fascinates, then threatens, forcing them to confront fears the familiar world let them avoid. What the special world really tests is the self: it is a mirror, and the protagonist must be changed by it to survive the return. The meaning of the whole turns on one question on the way home — has the protagonist truly grown, or was it 'just a dream' they will forget? Real growth makes the return a transformation; failure to learn makes the voyage merely an escape.

**The concept:** The strange new world is a mirror we can reach only by leaving home, and we follow the protagonist into it because distance is the one thing that lets us see clearly the life we were too close to before. It asks what we come to understand about where we belong only once we have left it behind.

**Examples:** Alice falling down the rabbit-hole into a Wonderland that fascinates then threatens, waking to ask whether it was only a dream — Alice's Adventures in Wonderland; the four children stepping through the wardrobe into Narnia and returning changed by all they have ruled and survived — The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe; Dorothy swept by the cyclone into Oz, longing through every wonder for the home she only truly sees once she is back — The Wonderful Wizard of Oz

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention crosses from the familiar into a strange world. · Feel: Wonder and disorientation resolve into relief. · Think: Strangeness changes the one who survives it. · Act: The reader returns to the familiar seeing it anew.

**How to analyse it:** Name the strange world entered and the ordinary one departed, then ask what the abnormal place lets the writer say about the normal one the protagonist must return to. Read Voyage and Return by the transformation the detour produces: the protagonist is changed by rules and dangers unlike home, so the writer’s idea often clarifies home by estranging it. Track what they carry back; Dorothy’s passage into Oz reorders her sense of home so completely that the return, not the marvels, becomes the point — she sees the ordinary world newly.

**How to create it:** Build the arc by launching the protagonist into a world with its own logic, letting its trials change them, then returning them altered to a home now seen differently. Use the strange world to comment on the familiar one. Caution: the return must matter — a voyage with no changed homecoming is a holiday, so make what they bring back reshape how they, and the reader, view the ordinary.

**Kinds of Voyage & Return** — One journey out and back told many ways — each a different strange world entered, and a different self brought home.

- **Kinds of voyage** — The other world the traveller enters, and how it changes them
  - **The Wonderland** — A fantastical world tests and teaches the traveller. _(e.g. Alice falling into a world that inverts her own — Alice in Wonderland)_
  - **The Survival Voyage** — A hostile place strips the traveller to essentials. _(e.g. the shipwrecked sailor rebuilding a life alone on an island — Robinson Crusoe)_
  - **The Cautionary Voyage** — The strange world is a mirror held up to the real one. _(e.g. Gulliver's travels satirising the society he left behind — Gulliver's Travels)_
  - **The Portal of Growth** — The traveller returns matured by the other world. _(e.g. the children who rule and grow in Narnia, then step back through the wardrobe — The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe)_

### Dramatic Situations `Ds`

Georges Polti's catalogue of the recurring conflicts beneath almost every story — from Supplication and Revenge to Mistaken Identity and Sacrifice. Each is not a plot shape but a predicament: a tight knot of opposed wants between people (a debt owed, a rivalry, a forbidden love, a secret that must out) that forces a defining choice. Where a plot structure such as Tragedy or the Quest gives a story its arc, a dramatic situation gives that arc its charged core, and one tale can layer several. Think of it as a menu of conflicts to enrich any structure, each chosen because it presses the protagonist into action that reveals who they are.

**The concept:** Every story we have ever felt turns out to rest on the same small set of human predicaments — supplication, vengeance, sacrifice — because these are the very situations our own lives keep placing us inside. To map them is to ask what it is in these few conflicts that has moved every audience unchanged across the centuries.

**Examples:** Lennie's mercy-killing as Sacrifice for an Ideal — the dream of safety defended by ending the one who endangers it — Of Mice and Men; Iago's Revenge for the lieutenancy denied him, pressing Othello toward the murder of what he loves — Othello; the lover's Crime of Love — strangling Porphyria to fix the perfect moment of her devotion forever — Porphyria's Lover

**Effect on the reader** — Focus: Attention names the core situation a story turns on. · Feel: We recognise the shape of the conflict. · Think: We see how one situation generates a whole plot. · Act: We test our own story against the catalogue.

**Polti’s 33 Dramatic Situations** — The recurring conflicts that underlie almost every story — open any one for its full range of forms

- **1 · Supplication** — a persecutor, a humble petitioner, and a power whose decision is in doubt
  - **A1** — Fugitives imploring the powerful for help against their enemies.
  - **A2** — Assistance implored for a pious duty which has been forbidden.
  - **A3** — An appeal for a refuge in which to die.
  - **B1** — Hospitality besought by the shipwrecked.
  - **B2** — Charity entreated by those cast off by their own people, whom they have disgraced.
  - **B3** — Expiation: the seeking of pardon, healing or deliverance.
  - **B4** — The surrender of a body or relic solicited.
  - **C1** — Supplication of the powerful for those dear to the suppliant.
  - **C2** — Supplication to a relative on behalf of another relative.
- **2 · Deliverance** — an unfortunate, a threatener, and a rescuer
  - **A** — Appearance of a rescuer to the condemned.
  - **B1** — A ruler restored to the throne by their children.
  - **B2** — Rescue by friends, or by strangers grateful for kindness shown.
- **3 · Crime Pursued by Vengeance** — an avenger and a criminal
  - **A1** — The avenging of a slain parent or ancestor.
  - **A2** — The avenging of a slain child or descendant.
  - **A4** — The avenging of a slain spouse.
  - **A7** — Vengeance for a slain or injured friend.
  - **B1** — Vengeance for deliberate injury or theft.
  - **B2** — Vengeance for having been despoiled in one's absence.
  - **B3** — Revenge for an attempted killing.
  - **B4** — Revenge for a false accusation.
  - **B6** — Vengeance for having been robbed of one's own.
  - **C** — Professional pursuit of criminals.
- **4 · Vengeance Taken for Kindred upon Kindred** — an avenging kinsman, a guilty kinsman, and the memory of the victim
  - **A1** — A parent's death avenged upon the other parent.
  - **B** — A brother's death avenged upon a son.
  - **C** — A parent's death avenged upon a spouse.
  - **D** — A spouse's death avenged upon a parent.
- **5 · Pursuit** — a punishment and a fugitive
  - **A** — Fugitives from justice pursued for crimes or political offences.
  - **C** — A hero struggling against a power.
  - **D** — One falsely thought mad, struggling against the one who would confine them.
- **6 · Disaster** — a vanquished power, and a victorious enemy or a messenger
  - **A1** — A defeat suffered.
  - **A2** — A homeland destroyed.
  - **A4** — A natural catastrophe.
  - **B** — A monarch overthrown.
  - **C1** — Ingratitude suffered.
  - **C2** — Unjust punishment or enmity endured.
  - **D1** — Abandonment by a spouse.
  - **D2** — Children lost by their parents.
- **7 · Falling Prey to Cruelty or Misfortune** — an unfortunate, and a master or a misfortune
  - **A** — The innocent made the victim of ambitious intrigue.
  - **B** — The innocent despoiled by those who should protect them.
  - **C1** — The powerful dispossessed and made wretched.
  - **C2** — A favourite or close friend finds themselves forgotten.
  - **D** — The unfortunate robbed of their last hope.
- **8 · Revolt** — a tyrant and a conspirator
  - **A1** — A conspiracy chiefly of one individual.
  - **A2** — A conspiracy of several.
  - **B1** — Revolt of one individual who influences and involves others.
  - **B2** — A revolt of many.
- **9 · Daring Enterprise** — a bold leader, an object, and an adversary
  - **A** — Preparations for war.
  - **B1** — War.
  - **B2** — Combat.
  - **C1** — Carrying off a desired person or object.
  - **C2** — Recapture of a desired object.
  - **D1** — An adventurous expedition.
  - **D2** — An adventure undertaken to win a beloved.
- **10 · Abduction** — the abductor, the abducted, and the guardian
  - **A** — The carrying-off of an unwilling captive.
  - **C1** — Recovery of the captive without the slaying of the abductor.
  - **D1** — Rescue of a captive friend.
  - **D2** — Rescue of a child.
- **11 · The Enigma** — an interrogator, a seeker, and a problem
  - **A** — Search for a person who must be found on pain of death.
  - **B** — A riddle to be solved on pain of death.
  - **C1** — Temptations offered to discover a hidden name.
  - **C3** — Tests to ascertain the state of someone's mind.
- **12 · Obtaining** — a solicitor and a refusing adversary, or an arbitrator between opposing parties
  - **A** — Efforts to obtain an object by cunning or force.
  - **B** — Endeavour by persuasive eloquence alone.
  - **C** — Eloquence before an arbitrator.
- **13 · Enmity of Kinsmen** — a malevolent kinsman, and a hated or mutually hating kinsman
  - **A1** — One sibling hated by several.
  - **A2** — Reciprocal hatred between siblings.
  - **A3** — Hatred between relatives for reasons of self-interest.
  - **B1** — Hatred of a child for a parent.
  - **B2** — Mutual hatred of parent and child.
  - **B3** — Hatred of a parent for a child.
  - **C** — Hatred of a grandparent for a grandchild.
  - **D** — Hatred between in-laws.
- **14 · Rivalry of Kinsmen** — the preferred kinsman, the rejected kinsman, and the object
  - **A1** — Malicious rivalry of a sibling.
  - **A2** — Malicious rivalry between two siblings.
  - **B1** — Rivalry of parent and child.
  - **C** — Rivalry of cousins.
  - **D** — Rivalry of friends.
- **15 · Madness** — a person in madness and a victim
  - **A1** — A kinsman harmed during madness.
  - **A2** — A spouse harmed during madness.
  - **A3** — Harm done, during madness, to a person not hated.
  - **B** — Disgrace brought upon oneself through madness.
  - **C** — Loss of loved ones brought about by madness.
  - **D** — Madness brought on by fear of inherited insanity.
- **16 · Fatal Imprudence** — the imprudent one, and the victim or the thing lost
  - **A1** — Imprudence the cause of one's own misfortune.
  - **A2** — Imprudence the cause of one's own disgrace.
  - **B1** — Curiosity the cause of one's own misfortune.
  - **B2** — Loss of a loved one through curiosity.
  - **C1** — Curiosity the cause of harm to others.
  - **C2** — Imprudence the cause of a relative's death.
  - **C5** — Credulity the cause of misfortune.
- **17 · Slaying of a Kinsman Unrecognised** — the slayer and the unrecognised victim
  - **A** — On the point of harming a daughter unknowingly, at the command of an oracle.
  - **B** — On the point of harming a son unknowingly.
  - **C** — On the point of harming a brother unknowingly.
  - **D** — A parent harmed unknowingly.
  - **E** — A parent insulted unknowingly.
  - **G** — Failure to rescue an unrecognised child.
- **18 · Self-Sacrifice for an Ideal** — the hero, the ideal, and the thing sacrificed
  - **A1** — Life sacrificed for the sake of one's word.
  - **A2** — Life sacrificed for the success of one's people.
  - **A3** — Life sacrificed for the happiness of one's people.
  - **A4** — Life sacrificed out of devotion to family.
  - **A5** — Life sacrificed for the sake of one's faith.
  - **B1** — Both love and life sacrificed for one's faith.
  - **B2** — Both love and life sacrificed for a cause.
  - **B3** — Love sacrificed to the interests of the state.
  - **C** — Well-being sacrificed to duty.
  - **D** — The ideal of honour sacrificed to the ideal of faith.
- **19 · Self-Sacrifice for Kindred** — the hero, the kinsman, and the thing sacrificed
  - **A1** — Life sacrificed for that of a relative or loved one.
  - **A2** — Life sacrificed for the happiness of a relative or loved one.
  - **B1** — Ambition sacrificed for the happiness of a parent.
  - **B2** — Ambition sacrificed for the life of a parent.
  - **C1** — Love sacrificed for the sake of a parent's life.
  - **C2** — Love sacrificed for the happiness of one's child.
  - **C3** — Love sacrificed for the happiness of a loved one.
  - **D1** — Life and honour sacrificed for the life of a loved one.
- **20 · All Sacrificed for a Passion** — the object of the fatal passion, and the thing sacrificed
  - **A2** — A vow broken for a passion.
  - **A5** — Power ruined by a passion.
  - **A6** — Ruin of mind, health and life.
  - **A8** — Ruin of fortunes, lives and honour.
  - **B** — Temptation destroying the sense of duty or pity.
  - **C1** — Destruction of honour, fortune and life by vice.
- **21 · Necessity of Sacrificing Loved Ones** — the hero, the beloved victim, and the necessity for the sacrifice
  - **A1** — The need to sacrifice a child in the public interest.
  - **A2** — The duty of sacrificing a child to fulfil a vow.
  - **A3** — The duty of sacrificing benefactors or loved ones to one's faith.
  - **B1** — The duty of sacrificing one's child under the pressure of necessity.
  - **B2** — The duty of sacrificing one's parent under the same pressure.
  - **B4** — The duty of sacrificing a kinsman for the public good.
- **22 · Rivalry of Superior and Inferior** — a superior rival, an inferior rival, and the object
  - **A1** — Rivalry of a mortal and an immortal.
  - **A4** — Rivalry of conqueror and conquered.
  - **A5** — Rivalry of victor and vanquished.
  - **A7** — Rivalry of usurper and subject.
  - **A9** — Rivalry of a powerful person and an upstart.
  - **A10** — Rivalry of rich and poor.
  - **A11** — Rivalry of an honoured man and a suspected one.
  - **A12** — Rivalry of two who are almost equal.
  - **B2** — Rivalry of victor and prisoner.
  - **B3** — Rivalry of a queen and a subject.
  - **B5** — Rivalry of a lady and her servant.
  - **C** — A rivalry in which several want the same thing.
- **23 · Discovery of the Dishonour of a Loved One** — the discoverer and the guilty one
  - **A1** — Discovery of a parent's shame.
  - **A2** — Discovery of one's child's disgrace.
  - **A3** — Discovery of a sibling's disgrace.
  - **B1** — Discovery of disgrace in the family of one's betrothed.
  - **C** — Discovery that one's child has killed.
  - **D1** — The duty of punishing one's child, a traitor to their country.
  - **D3** — The duty of punishing a child believed guilty.
- **24 · Obstacles to Marriage** — two suitors and an obstacle
  - **A1** — Marriage prevented by inequality of rank.
  - **A2** — Inequality of fortune an impediment to marriage.
  - **B** — Marriage prevented by enemies or by chance obstacles.
  - **C** — Marriage forbidden because of a previous betrothal.
  - **D1** — A marriage impeded by the opposition of relatives.
  - **D2** — Family affection disturbed by the in-laws.
- **25 · An Enemy Loved** — the beloved enemy, the lover, and the hater
  - **A1** — The loved one hated by the family of the husband or wife.
  - **A3** — The spouse hated by the family of the other.
  - **A4** — The spouse belongs to the enemy party.
  - **B1** — The lover is the slayer of the father of the beloved.
  - **B6** — The lover has slain a kinsman of the beloved.
- **26 · Ambition** — an ambitious person, a thing coveted, and an adversary
  - **A1** — Ambition watched and guarded against by a kinsman or loyal friend.
  - **A2** — Ambition watched by a relative or a person under obligation.
  - **A3** — Ambition watched by partisans.
  - **B** — Rebellious ambition, akin to Revolt.
  - **C1** — Ambition and greed heaping crime upon crime.
  - **C2** — Ambition that destroys even a parent.
- **27 · Conflict with a God** — a mortal and an immortal
  - **A1** — Struggle against a deity.
  - **A2** — Strife with the believers in a god.
  - **B1** — Controversy with a deity.
  - **B2** — Punishment for contempt of a god.
  - **B3** — Punishment for pride before a god.
  - **B4** — Presumptuous rivalry with a god.
- **28 · Mistaken Jealousy** — the jealous one, the object of jealousy, the supposed rival, and the cause of the mistake
  - **A1** — The mistake arising in the suspicious mind of the jealous one.
  - **A2** — Jealousy aroused by a fatal chance.
  - **A4** — Baseless jealousy aroused by malicious rumours.
  - **B1** — Jealousy suggested by a traitor moved by hatred.
  - **B2** — Jealousy suggested by a traitor moved by self-interest.
- **29 · Erroneous Judgement** — the mistaken one, the victim, the cause of the mistake, and the truly guilty
  - **A1** — Suspicion where faith is needed.
  - **A2** — A false suspicion.
  - **A4** — False suspicion aroused by a misunderstood gesture.
  - **B1** — False suspicion drawn upon oneself to save a friend.
  - **B2** — False suspicion falling upon the innocent.
  - **B5** — False suspicion falling upon one who believes themselves guilty.
  - **C1** — The accusation allowed to fall upon an enemy.
  - **D1** — False suspicion thrown by the real culprit upon an enemy.
  - **D6** — The struggle to clear one's name after a deliberate miscarriage of justice.
- **30 · Remorse** — the culprit, the victim or the sin, and the questioner
  - **A1** — Remorse for an unknown crime.
  - **A2** — Remorse for the killing of a parent.
  - **A3** — Remorse for an assassination.
  - **A4** — Remorse for a wrongful conviction one has caused.
  - **A5** — Remorse for the death of a spouse.
- **31 · Recovery of a Lost One** — the seeker and the one found
  - **A** — The recovery of a loved one long lost.
- **32 · Loss of Loved Ones** — a kinsman lost, a kinsman who witnesses, and the agent of the loss
